=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string caption of length n. A good caption is a string where every character appears in groups of at least 3 consecutive occurrences.
For example:

"aaabbb" and "aaaaccc" are good captions.
"aabbb" and "ccccd" are not good captions.

You can perform the following operation any number of times:
Choose an index i (where 0 <= i < n) and change the character at that index to either:

The character immediately before it in the alphabet (if caption[i] != 'a').
The character immediately after it in the alphabet (if caption[i] != 'z').

Your task is to convert the given caption into a good caption using the minimum number of operations, and return it. If there are multiple possible good captions, return the lexicographically smallest one among them. If it is impossible to create a good caption, return an empty string "".
 
Example 1:

Input: caption = "cdcd"
Output: "cccc"
Explanation:
It can be shown that the given caption cannot be transformed into a good caption with fewer than 2 operations. The possible good captions that can be created using exactly 2 operations are:

"dddd": Change caption[0] and caption[2] to their next character 'd'.
"cccc": Change caption[1] and caption[3] to their previous character 'c'.

Since "cccc" is lexicographically smaller than "dddd", return "cccc".

Example 2:

Input: caption = "aca"
Output: "aaa"
Explanation:
It can be proven that the given caption requires at least 2 operations to be transformed into a good caption. The only good caption that can be obtained with exactly 2 operations is as follows:

Operation 1: Change caption[1] to 'b'. caption = "aba".
Operation 2: Change caption[1] to 'a'. caption = "aaa".

Thus, return "aaa".

Example 3:

Input: caption = "bc"
Output: ""
Explanation:
It can be shown that the given caption cannot be converted to a good caption by using any number of operations.

 
Constraints:

1 <= caption.length <= 5 * 10^4
caption consists only of lowercase English letters.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minCostGoodCaption(self, caption: str) -> str:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Can we reduce? Need compute g(i,c,k) for all c,k. Recurrence:
For k=0 (current run length 1):
  Only same char x=c allowed (since cannot switch before length>=3). So:
  g[i][c][0] = dist(i,c) + g[i+1][c][1].
For k=1:
  Only same char x=c allowed:
  g[i][c][1] = dist(i,c) + g[i+1][c][2].
For k=2 (current run length >=3):
  Can continue same char x=c:
    dist(i,c) + g[i+1][c][2]
  Or switch to any x != c:
    dist(i,x) + g[i+1][x][0]
  So:
  g[i][c][2] = min( dist(i,c)+g[i+1][c][2],
                    min_{x != c} dist(i,x)+g[i+1][x][0] )
This is much faster: for k=0,1 O(26) per i; for k=2 need for each c min over x != c of A[x] = dist(i,x)+g[i+1][x][0]. We can compute global smallest and second smallest of A[x] over x; then for each c:
  if best_x != c use best; else use second best.
Thus O(26*3 + 26) per i ~ O(26n), not 26^2. Great.

Time: Computing DP with array accesses. Need optimize local variables.

Let's write code:

from array import array

class Solution:
    def minCostGoodCaption(self, caption: str) -> str:
        n = len(caption)
        if n < 3:
            return ""
        s = [ord(ch)-97 for ch in caption]
        INF = 10**9
        total = (n+1)*78
        g = array('i', [INF]) * total
        # set g[n] k2 block to 0
        base_n = n*78
        for c in range(26):
            g[base_n + 52 + c] = 0
        # compute backwards
        for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
            orig = s[i]
            nb = (i+1)*78
            cb = i*78
            # compute A best and k0,k1
            best1 = INF
            best2 = INF
            best_char = -1
            # local g variable
            for c in range(26):
                d = orig - c
                if d < 0: d = -d
                # A = d + g[nb + c]
                val = d + g[nb + c]
                if val < best1:
                    best2 = best1
                    best1 = val
                    best_char = c
                elif val < best2:
                    best2 = val
                # k0 = d + g[nb+26+c]
                g[cb + c] = d + g[nb + 26 + c]
                # k1 = d + g[nb+52+c]
                g[cb + 26 + c] = d + g[nb + 52 + c]
            # compute k2
            for c in range(26):
                d = orig - c
                if d < 0: d = -d
                cont = d + g[nb + 52 + c]
                switch = best1 if best_char != c else best2
                g[cb + 52 + c] = cont if cont < switch else switch
        # start
        best_cost = INF
        for x in range(26):
            d = s[0] - x; if d<0...
            val = d + g[1*78 + x]  # k0 block offset x
            if val < best_cost: best_cost = val
        if best_cost >= INF: return ""
        # greedy
        res = []
        cur_c = -1
        cur_k = -1
        used = 0
        for i in range(n):
            orig = s[i]
            nb = (i+1)*78
            chosen = -1
            for x in range(26):
                if i == 0:
                    newc = x
                    newk = 0
                else:
                    if x == cur_c:
                        newc = cur_c
                        if cur_k < 2:
                            newk = cur_k + 1
                        else:
                            newk = 2
                    else:
                        if cur_k != 2:
                            continue
                        newc = x
                        newk = 0
                d = orig - x; if d<0: d=-d
                # g index: nb + newk*26 + newc
                if used + d + g[nb + newk*26 + newc] == best_cost:
                    chosen = x
                    break
            # chosen should not be -1
            res.append(chr(97+chosen))
            used += abs(orig-chosen)
            if i == 0:
                cur_c = chosen
                cur_k = 0
            else:
                if chosen == cur_c:
                    if cur_k < 2:
                        cur_k += 1
                    # else stays 2
                else:
                    cur_c = chosen
                    cur_k = 0
        return ''.join(res)

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "minimum operations" if there is a run of length 3 and the optimal char for that run is not the median because of lexicographic tie? Wait, DP minimizes cost first, so for a fixed run, cost is minimized by median. But lexicographic tie can choose a non-median char if same total cost? If a non-median char has higher cost for that run, it could be compensated by lower cost in another run? The total cost is sum; DP considers all combinations. Lexicographic tie only among equal total cost. So a non-median char in one run might be part of an optimal total if it allows a much better next run? But run costs are independent except run length boundaries and char constraints (adjacent runs must differ). Choosing a different char for a run could affect whether it equals next run char? Wait adjacent runs must have different chars. If you choose a non-median char for current run, it might allow the next run to use a char that is the same as current? No, next run must be different from current. The cost of next run depends on its char, but not on current char except it cannot equal current. So current char choice can constrain next run's char, potentially affecting cost. Thus a non-median current char could be optimal globally if it allows a cheaper next run? Actually if current char is different from next char, the next char cannot equal current. If current char is median, it might block a very cheap next char that equals median. But since next run must be different, choosing current char to be something else could unblock that cheap next char. However, the cost of current run increases, but next run decreases. DP handles. Good.
